The meaning of the stanza: Many heads of men are visible, and all of them are hairy, and all are large, and they are fastened just onto necks, not taken in the hand like a tāla fruit. There is no differentiation among them by these qualities. But here, “who among you is discerning?” he said with reference to himself. Kaṇṇavā means wise. But there is no one who doesn’t have an ear-hole. Hearing this, those youths became silent, shame-faced, shoulders slumped, downcast, scratching the ground with their fingers.

Then, seeing their shamelessness, the teacher explained the riddle: ‘Learn the riddle, dears.’ Kāla means both before-meal time and after-meal time, and so on. Bhūtāni is a designation for beings. Time does not eat the skin and flesh of beings, but swallows and devours them by depriving them of life, beauty, and strength by crushing their youth, and by destroying their health. “Together with itself”: thus devouring them, it does not omit anything but devours all.
Not only does it devour all beings, but it also devours itself.
For the earlier part of the day does not remain when the later part arrives.
Similarly in the times of the later meal and so on.
"He who has become a devourer of time" is an epithet for the one whose āsavas are destroyed ( khīṇāsava).
For he is called one who “swallows up time” (kālaghaso) because he has “eaten up” time by barring out the possibility of future rebirth (paṭisandhi-kāla).
"He consumes the consumer of beings" means: it is craving (taṇhā) which consumes beings in the planes of misery (apāya). This the arhat has burnt up with the fire of knowledge (ñāṇa-aggi) and reduced to ashes. Thence he is said to consume the consumer of beings’ (bhūta-pacaniṃ pacī).
There is such a reading as ‘‘pajani’’.
janikaṃ nibbattikaṃ means generating and producing.
Time devours beings, devours all with itself, and he who has become the devourer of time, he consumes the consumer of beings. Then those youths, having seen the meaning of the question clearly, like the smooth and rough parts of the road illuminated at night by the light of a thousand lamps, [given] by the teacher's explanation (vissajjanena), said: “Now we, as long as life lasts, will live in the teacher's residence (guru-vāsaṃ). We will live, these teachers are truly great. We are people who generate conceit because of much learning (bahussuta-mānaṃ), but we do not even know the meaning of a four-line stanza (catuppadikagāthāya).” Humbled, from then on they performed their proper duties (vatta-paṭipattiṃ) towards their teacher as before, and were intent on heaven (sagga-parāyaṇā).
I, monks (bhikkhave), at that time, was the teacher of those [youths], these monks (bhikkhū) were youths (māṇavakā).
Thus previously, I, these, thus of heads held high with pride (mānapaggahitasire) wandered around, you did not subdue my pride.
Having heard this jātaka, those monks (bhikkhū) said: "Previously as well, we were ruined by pride (mānena)," becoming even more humble, they applied themselves to their individual meditation subjects (upakāraka-kammaṭṭhāna-parāyaṇā).
Then the Blessed One (bhagavā), at one time, while wandering on tour through the country, reached Vesālī, and dwelling at the Gotamaka shrine (cetiya), knowing the maturity of understanding (ñāṇa-paripākaṃ) of those five hundred monks (bhikkhūnaṃ), he taught this Gotamaka Sutta – “Through direct knowledge (abhiññāya), monks (bhikkhave), I teach the Dharma (dhammaṃ), not without direct knowledge; I am with a cause (sa-nidāna)… etc… I am with convincing power (sappāṭihāriya), monks (bhikkhave), I teach the Dharma (dhammaṃ), not without convincing power (appāṭihāriyaṃ).
For me, monks (bhikkhave), teaching the Dharma (dhammaṃ) through direct knowledge (abhiññāya)… etc… not without convincing power (appāṭihāriyaṃ).
My exhortation (ovāda) should be put into practice, my instruction (anusāsanī) should be put into practice.
It is sufficient for you to be pleased (tuṭṭhiyā), monks (bhikkhave), sufficient for you to be exultant (attamanatāya), sufficient for you to be joyful (somanassāya).
The perfectly enlightened Buddha (sammā-saṃbuddho) is the Blessed One (bhagavā), the Dharma (dhammo) is well-proclaimed (svākkhāto), the Saṅgha is practicing the good path (suppaṭipanno."
The Blessed One (bhagavā) said this. And while this exposition (veyyākaraṇasmiṃ) was being spoken, the ten thousandfold world system (dasa-sahassi-lokadhātu) shook (A.
Ni.
3.
126).
Having heard this sutta, those five hundred monks (bhikkhū) in that very seat (āsane) attained arahatship together with the discriminations (paṭisambhidāhi), thus this teaching reached its completion (niṭṭhamagamāsi).
The Eighth Chapter on the Tathāgata’s Way is Concluded.
The Mūlapariyāyasutta commentary in the Papañcasūdanī, the commentary to the Majjhimanikāya, is concluded.
